and when a political officer was in 1868 stationed in Bik4nir, an
effort was made to obtain fuller details regarding this important
Saive ye (he. great tribe, the head of which was, after the Amer.
aipur vief, and his son, the Hindi ig :
Soutcf Abba. of highest rank at the
_ AS Kiyént,” or history of Bikdnir, had been recently com. :
_ piled by Diyalji, one of the principal Chaérans, or bards of the state,
but it was many months before a copy of it could’ be procured,
It. proved to be a manuscript of formidable bulk, and comprised
the history of the Rathor tribe from its origin, but as the copying
had to be completed quickly, only that part was transcribed which: '
related to Bikanir, and which began with the career of Bikd.

The language of the work was the dialect of Bikanir, includ- —
ing some Urdi words, and though wvitten in prose, it was
garnished with long extracts from old poems in verification of .
the facts narrated, with which they are usually contemporaneous .
or nearly so. Single couplets (doha), too, are often quoted in
attestation of a date or an event. Diyalji’s work is generally
too long, tautological and involved, to be translated at length; .
but anecdotes are often well told, and terse colloquialisms often
met with. The extracts in verse I have read very little of,
because I found many of the words in them could only be
interpreted by a Bikdniri, and I had only a Shekhawati tutor,
but the whole of the prose which forms the bulk of the work |
T have read and translated. Shorter works fell into my hands —
regarding Bika and the Charani woman, Karniji, an incarnation —
of Mahadevi, who is believed even by the most travelled and -
intelligent men of the present day in Bikdnir to have bestowed
the territory on her protégé Bika.

The reign of Raji Antip Singh, perhaps the golden time of
Bikénir valour and fame, was the subject of another little book,
which fell into my hands, and no doubt many such might be
obtained. I have, however, in the following pages given in an -
abbreviated form the version of Diydlji, the official bard, only
adding a few comments where they seemed necessary.

The limits within which these poems—for, as I have stated .
above, the Kiydnt is based on old poems—can be looked upon as ©
affording data for history, are-explained by Colonel Tod. They
touch on little besides war, marriage, and religious observances,
and where.the military character of favourite warriors are con- |
cerned, they are'full of extravagances: suppressions are frequent,
and liberality to Charans is no doubt exaggerated for the sake of
the precedent. But they often expose ingratitude and crime
boldly, they are not inaccurate as regards the general narrative,
though the order of events is often wrong, and, like all national:
ballads, while deriving their.own character from the spirit of the
=== PDFPAGE 0008 ===
Ml

people, they re-act on the latter, and are a better guide to their
feelings and interests than more matter-of-fact histories would
be. The story of Bikdnfr, as told by Diydlji, is believed by Bika-
nirfs, and it may be more advantageous to those who have social
intercourse with Rajputs, and more practically useful to them in
giving an insight into Rajput character to have some acquaint-
ance with the history of the land as believed in by the people
themselves, tedious though it may be, than to possess a critical
knowledge of it derived from many collated manuscripts. I have

greatly abbreviated the narrative as given in the Kiydnt, but the
fear of tediousness has not deterred me from inserting anything
which, I thought, might be useful in any way. I believe verna-

cular prose histories of states, intended to be complete and
. based to a great extent on old records and poems, as well as on
. tradition, are rare. Indeed, I have known a Charan poet dis-
tinctly decline to write prose as being infra dig.

Bikdénir has a reputation in Rajputdnd for its collection of
books, and from the time of Bika there has been a succession of
local poets whose works have been to a great extent preserved.

In Sambat 1753 (A. D, 1696) one Jasrap Mundra of Bikénir
made a collection of them, and his compilation, a ponderous
volume, is in the Bikénir palace library. In the time of Ajit
Singh of Jodhpur, one Nainsukh Munot of that state composed a
Kfydnt or history | of the Rathors, which is also in the Bikénfr

palace. I have, since the Gazetteer was printed, ascertained that
these two books furnished the basis of Diydljis Kiyént. He
quotes nearly fifty poems up to the death of Andp Singh, of
which the authors of more than thirty are specified, and are for
the most part known persons :-—

be taken possession of by the Réthors without dispute. .This, however, was
but a small tract. To its north and west the Bhatis ruled, and to the east,
north-east, and south-east, were the settlements of independent Jats ; beyond the.
Jats round Bhatner were Bh&tis, Chayals and Johyds, chiefly, if not entirely,
Muhammadans. Hisshr was occupied by the Dehli Emperor’s Stibaddr. The
Kdéim Khénis held what is now Shekhawati. The Bidawat country was in the
possession of the Mobil Rajputs, and the tract in the east where Reni, is
situated was occupied by Chayal.and Khinchi Rajputs. The present Bikdnfr
territory was, according to Cunningham, originally called Bagardesh, from
one Bagri Rado, who probably founded Bégor, which, according to Tod, Bik&
took from the Bh&tis. ;
Bika the first day of his march reached Mandaur, and going to the shrine

of Goreji devoted himself to his work. On rising
the following morning he found Bhairdji’s*
amulet (murat) amongst his amulets, although it
was not there the night before. Niapo, the Sankhl4, remarked, ‘ Goreji + goes
- with you, your ‘raj’ will indeed be strong.” Thus encouraged, Bikd set off, _
and-on reaching. Deshnik, 16 miles south of the present city of Bikdnir, he
paid his respects to a famous Chéran woman named Karniji, who was known
to be gifted with supernatural power. She said to him, “ Your destiny is
higher than your fathers, and many servants will touch your feet.” In obe-
dience to the direction of Karniji, Bika first settled at Chindfsar, where he
abode three years; be then came and dwelt six years at Deshntik, where he
frequently waited on Karniji. After that he lived for three years at Ko-
Bik& lives successively at ramdesar, near Gajnér, where, perceiving it was a
Chindésar, Deshnik, Koram- suitable place, he set up the image of Goreji on 2
desar, Janglu, tank embankment; the temple is a place of some
note at the present day, and there are traces of Bika’s small fort. He afterwards’
dwelt ten years at Janglu, which he converted from a depopulated into a
flourishing district. His 100 horse had now grown into 400, composed of
Biké marries the daughter of Rajputs of various tribes. It was whilst he dwelt
the Pagal chief. 8 at Jangli that Karniji signified her wish that he .

should marry into a family of local consequence.
That of Shekho, the Bh&ti R4o of Ptigal in the desert to the north, was fixed

upon; for Shekho had been styled “ brother” and blessed by Karnfjf, whom he -
came to pay his respects to on the fourteenth of each fortnight. On her.
signifying her desire to Shekho, he refused, and was even unmoved by her
prophecy that thousands good as he should touch the feet of Biké. Now Shekho
was a great robber. One day he was returning with booty from the-neigh-.
bourhood of Multdn, when he was overtaken by 400 of the Multdn garrison,
who pub his followers to flight and carried him captive.to the Subadér of
Multan, who confined him in a matchlock-defended fort. Shekho’s lady went
to beg Karniji to help her “ brother,” promising that his daughter Rang Kaur’
should marry Bika if the prayer were granted. Karnfjf, however, insisted that
arrangements for the marriage should’ be made at once, though she promised
that Shekho should be back in time for it. Accordingly Gogli, Shekho’s .
manager, was employed, and everything was prepared. ~ - ‘

Biké on the appointed day repaired to Pigal with his marriage party, and:
there, too, Karniji presented herself. The marriage proceeded, and when they
came to the circuit ceremony, and the presence of the bride’s father was
necessary, Karnjji flew off to Multan, brought Shekho and caused him ‘to ©

The explorers journeyed until they came to Rétf Ghatf, a rocky and
raviny spot on the track between Ndgor and
Mult&n. There, in a “ Khar” thicket, they beheld
a sheep with two lambs, which several wolves were after. But the sheep fear-
lessly faced them whenever they ‘approached to seize her young, and by her
bold front kept them at a distance.

N&po drove away the wolves and at once pronounced the spot auspicious,
Word was sent to Biké, who came and laid the
foundations of a fort. ‘this, however, was not the
present fort of Bikénfr; but the future site of the latter was also mdicated about
the same time as follows. N&po and one Naro had again gone to observe omens.
As they rose early in the morning, they noticed 2 man asleep close by with his
head resting upon a tuft of “bharit” grass which served him as a pillow,
Round the tuft was coiled a poisonous snake, which the man who had Jain down
in the darkness had not noticed. Népo directed that the reptile should not be
molested, but carefully watched. When it moved away they followed it, and
it led them to the ground already pointed out by the incident of the wolves
and sheep, N&po then foretold the crection of the present and larger fort on

. the spot where the snake had first been seen. The

party of Bikdvtr founded, Sam- fort of Biké was founded in Sambat 1542; and

The Godfrdés voluntarily acknowledged the sovereignty of Bika, and the
heads of their clan still place the tiké on the
Godéra Jats still place “tiki” forehead of every new occupant of the “gadi”

on f new occupant of the Bika. of Bika.

pir “Gadi.” The circumstances which led to this

; voluntary submission is not noticed by the author
of the “ Kiydnt,” which is my chief source of information. But, although he
ignores the ‘act ‘that the Godarés are not Bikantr subjects by right of conquest,
it is admitted in Bikdnir, and the reasons of their acceptance of Biké as their
sovereign lord are mentioned by Tod. Their object was protection from
Johyés. and Bhatis * and immunity from the plundering incursions of the
Rathors. An incident which showed the Godards the advantage of possessing
so powerful a protector, also illustrates the social life of the simple Jats of the
desert at this period, and I will relate it as much as possible in the words of
the Kiyant. :

Pandit, the Chief or Chaudhri of the Godéras, had a great reputation for —
liberality. One day a “ Dadhi’’} of -the Godérds
came begging to Palo, the Chaudhri of the Sarans.
Piilo gave her what he could afford, and afterwards drank wine and went
to sleep in his house. Meanwhile ‘his wife Maliki, a daughter of Réisél,
head of the Beniwils, heard of the circumstance, and apparently think-
ing that the Dadhi had not been treated bountifully enough, went and —
begged her husband to- exhibit such bounty to the beggar that it should
eclipse that of Pandé and excite the wonder of the Godards, amongst. whom

was the “ DAdhi’s home.” Piilo, angry at being disturbed, and full of liquor,
exclaimed, “ Widow, be off to Pandu if you like,” and he struck his wife -
severaltimes. She, enraged at the outrage, exclaimed that henceforth his bed
should be to her as her brother’s, and she took an oath not to speak to
her husband again. She then shut herself up in the house and obstinately
held to her vow. This continued for six months, and the quarrel between the
Chaudhri and Chaudhran became known throughout the clan. After that
period the brotherhood assembled, in order to effect a reconciliation between:
their chief and his lady. They first lulled goats, sent’ for liquor, stretched the
festive (pantia) cloth and enjoyed a feast. They then sent a messenger to .—

The Mohil country lay along part of the present Jaipur boundary, included
Lédno to the south of Stijangarh, and is snid to have consisted of 1,400 villages.
Of these, the principal were Dronpur and Chfpar, which at one time cave
their names to the tract. Chapar is situated on a salt lake, a few miles north
of Sijingarh, and Dronpur was under a hill, the “K4l4 Dungar,” within ten
Legendary history of the miles and to the west of Stjéngarh. Legends tell
Mohil country, the present that to this hill, Dron4, the famous Bréhman tutor of
Biddwati. the Pandus, retired, built.a city and ruled the adjacent
territory. Subsequently, the district came into the possession of. the Dahiliya
Panwar Réjptits, who, itis said, ruled it for 1,500 years. They were ex--
pelled by the Bagri R4jpiits from Ndgor, who remained 1,900 years. The
Mohils under Réné Sajan followed, and they, according to tradition, held sway
for 619 years. Mohil, it is stated, was seventh in descent from the fire-born
Chohiin, but it does not appear how many generations lie between Mohil
too, and the conqueror of Chépar Dronpur. So “far is

noes on the Mohils by mythological. Probable history now begins. In the -

no time of Jodhd, chief of Méarwar, the head of. the..
Mohils was Ajit, and to him Jodh& married one of his daughters. In spite “~
of his alliance, however, Jodh4 coveted the Mohil lands for one of his sons,
and taking advantage of a visit of Ajit to Jodhpur, he ‘resolved to slay him,
although he was his guest and son-in-law. Ajit’s mother-in-law, however,
became aware of the plot and sent word to Ajit’s “ pésbén” (mistress) to
warn Ajit that he must not stay a single “ghari” longer (half an hour).
The “ pésbéin ” informed Ajit’s minister (pardhdn), who reflected that Ajit was
not the man to fly from danger; so that to induce him'to‘ leave Jodhpur at
once, stratagem must be resorted to. Accordingly, Ajit was informed that
news had just arrived of an attack on Chapar by Jats, and that succour was
urgently needed to save the town. Then Ajit sounded his’“ nagéra” (kettle-
drum), and without further notice rode off. At village Ganero, not- very far
from Chdpar, Ajit perceived that a force was following him, and on’ enquiry

the whole story of the plot and counterplot was told; and he learnt that

Jodha, guessing that his treachery had been exposed, had followed him, to
attempt by force what be had failed to effect by fraud. Ajit, displeased at
being tricked into retreat, angrily pulled his mustache and prepared-for the .
fight, which followed, and which was fierce. ‘Losses on each side were heavy ;
but, Ajit being slain, the victory remained with Jodha, who not strong enough
io follow up his success returned to Jodhpur, and his daughter became “ sati”
mm the funeral pile of her slain husband. (For: another cause of the feud
yetween the Rathors and Mohils, vzde Tod, vol..I, page 632.) 7

inally compelled him to abandon the country. Meght retained. his power. -

Ram Lal Dwaérkani, appointed in A: D..1856, was a really good minister, °

Diwan Rém Lél All classes speak of his period of'-office as a golden
° , time, when nobles were treated with due considera-

tion, when the complaints of the poor were listened to patiently, and if justice
was not cheap to those who could pay for it,.it was at least obtainable. He »
enjoyed the support of a-powerful Rani, and had consequently much greater. .
freedom of action than is usually possessed by Bikdnir ministers. But the’
Rani died in A. D. 1861, after which Rém Lél’s enemies prevailed against
him, and he died in disgrace, some say by violence. ws —

PART IL.

STATISTICAL ACCOUNT.

.

—_ jptit state of Bikdnir is situated between 27° 30’

Bickain—The Rath 55’ latitude and 72° 30’ and 75° 40’ longitude.

GrxgRAL Descriprioy. Its area* is about 23,500 square miles, and its

4 Testione estimated population about 800,000. It is bounded

‘Water in Békénir is found, notwithstanding the slight apparent difference -

Water. - in the level of the country, at very varying depths
Ce and of quality: as unequal. - Thus. the city wells
are-more than three hundred feet deep, but the water of most is of excellent

=== PDFPAGE 0106 ===
( 83 ) —

in the hot-weather, and that below is injurious, At the village of Nokha,
south of the city on the Mérwér border, Dr. Moore found a well four hundred
feet deep. He makes the following remarks regarding it :—

“ Water when drawn from this well was quite hot. No one seemed to
“have any clear idea when the well was made, and no one knew why
that’particular spot was fixed upon for sinking a well. In other places
“the one village well is generally in a hollow or depression between the
“sand hills, where rt would be reasonable to expect water nearer the sur-
“face. But Nokha is on a flat site, rather elevated above the surrounding
“country, the ground hard, and not at all the locality where water would
“be sought for at that depth with the confident expectation of reward for
the labor. It was stated that no one attempts to sink such wells now,
“ probably because the number existing proves sufficient for the wants of the
“inhabitants, and not, as it would appear, from absence of workmen, for
“ during our stay men were sent down to clear the bottom, so that a better

- “ supply of tater might be obtained. The shaft being so narrow, the cleans-
“ing process is rather a tedious business: first a basket, is let down, then
‘a man; he fills the basket at the bottom of the well; he is then drawn up,
* and afterwards the basket with its contents; and so on till the well is
“cleaned out. The material brought up was sandy mud, having the usual
“earthy odour. The water allowed to clear was good, but hard from excess
“of lime.”

Along the Jaiptir border the water is generally of the best quality, and
in some places famous for its salubrious properties; but for the most part
throughout the state a newly dug well is as likely to produce bad as good
water, and it sometimes is so bad as actually to kill, by excessive irritation

a tank or depression.

‘valuable of grazing shrubs, is plentiful ina few places.
Of grasses the “ bharit” is generally the commonest.in the southern half -

Grasses. seed) is more eaten by the very poor than any
other kind of grain, and it supplies fairly good fodder for cattle, it may be
considered a very valuable production. But its-prickly husks lame dogs,
are “the torment of travellers,” and add appreciably to the discomforts of a
residence in Bikdnfr—a circumstance which. in times past has been. turned

All Bikénfr may be said to be ® pasture ground, To the north about
H4niimfngath (the old Bhatner) the grazing is famous, but after fair rains

The people of Réjmiténd sometimes divide the country into botanical

zones, the limits of which are sufficiently defined to form rough political
boundaries: thus the “lai” and the ‘sajji” mark the desert, the “ bhartt??
grass the country south, the *‘ karr”’ grass still farther south, and the “aonla”
bush marks the fertile region of Mewér south of that (ride History, pages 15
and 86, and Tod, Vol. I, page 595, 2nd edition).
‘In the northern part of the state west of Andpgath there is little
° cultivation, and the people depend chiefly on their
cattle, which are watered at reservoirs called
“tobas” and graze at large. Sindhis and others bring their cattle to this
tract when grass is plentiful; but a clan of Pirz4dds, or descendants of a
Muhammadan_ saint, claim to be the lords of the region between Antipgath,
Piigal, and Marot, having obtained it in grant from the Emperor of Dehli.
The robbers of the desert respected their persons and property as they would
have respected no one else: the Bikanfr Darbér exacts no dues from them, and
they themselves receive tio seers of ghi from all who pasture their cattle in
the waste (see Captain Jackson’s report on Bikénfr and Bhiwalpur boundary).
The Réths (Mussalméns of Réjpiit extraction) of this region are spoken of as
a pastoral tribe,

Where water is procurable, deer are common enough, and there are two
peculiar kinds. In the neighborhood of Gajner
wild pigs are preserved and do great mischief,
No life may be taken near the temples of repute; and as they are always
beside tanks, wild animals can drink unmolested. Wolves abound to the
north of Bikanfr and do much mischief. The inhabitants when they kill
them sometimes take their heads to Sirsé for the reward-given by the British
Government. Foxes are common and easily approached, and snakes are
numérous, Rats are abundant and troublesome,

Partridges (but J believe only the brown kind) are very common in Biki-
nit. ‘The largest coveys are to be seen in and about the villages. The large
bustard {giran) is always to be found within a few miles of the city of
Bikén{r, and is often met with elsewhere; as also are several kinds of grouse
(Latbat, &e.).. Florican (¢ilor) are numerous in some places, and at Koldth,
Gajner and the tanks of that region wild ducks are abundant in the cold-
weather,

A complete account of the plants and animals of the desert and sandy
tract is not in existence. It is to be hoped that some person with the
requisite leisure and opportunity will prepare one, and also amplify Colonel
Tod’s general account, which however has reference more to the desert west
of Bikanfr than to that within its limits,

The Jats, who are more than twice as numerous as any other caste, were

Tite anciently the possessors of the greater portion of

. the present Bikdnir territory. Some account of

their position, former organization, and subjugation will ‘be Yound in the
history of Biké’s occupation of the country. They are the agriculturists of
the country, and more severely taxed than any other class. A Jt is said to
pay thirteen taxes of different sorts, Many of them are Bishnawis and eschew
the taking of life. They are generally civil and obliging, but will not assist or
sanction the slaughter of game. They are good-humoured and ready to joke
about the rats which do so much mischief to their crops and stores of grain.
Notwithstanding the weight of taxation laid on them, they are attached to the
Mahfrdjé, and_so proud of notice.from him that s J&t has been known to
present a “nazar” of Rs. 1,200 in order to secure an extra share. The
headman of the Godéra clan of J&ts places the “ tilak” (or mark of inangura.
tion) on the forehead of every Raj& of Bfkénir when he first takes his seat on
the adi.” ‘The practice has prevailed ever since the foundation of the rij.
The Bishnawi Jats bury instead of bumine their dead, and “sat{s”’ are some.
times buried alive with the corpses of their husbands. Pip4sar and Samrathal
are mentioned in the Hissér Gazetteer as places in Bik4nir sacred ag being

The worship of Karnijf, a Charan woman who was an incarnation of

eee Devt, is the chief religiuus deity of the court, It was

Temple of Kerniji. her supernatural power which secured the territory
of Bik&nfr to Biké and his descendants, and were she estranged the state would
be ruined. The temple of Deshnok, as old as the state, 18 therefore the chief
shrine in Bikénir; but Karniji is invoked in other lands far from the place of
her origin, and she has devotecs of all castes. Some temples in her honor
were bmlt in the Dakhan, aud one raised by a Sesodia lady is in the upper
fort at Alwar. As represented on stones or on gold and silver charms worn
round the neck, Karniji holds a  tristil” or trident in her hand.

Lakshm{, however, is in Bikanfr worshipped with scarcely Jess devotion
than Karniji. The temple of Lakshmi N&th is

About twenty-five acres can be ploughed each season: pair of
bullocks. Camels are used as much as, and in many places mc fe tae
to draw the plough, and buffaloes and even. donkeys, especially in Antipgarh are
used for the same purpose. A fair crop of bajré yields five British maunds __
to the acre ; but I am unable to give the out-turn for different kinds of |
lands, Generally speaking, the quality of the - cultivated soil. varies but
little, for it is so hght that the surface is always shifting from the effects of —
the wind, and the fine blown deposits, which act as manure,.tend to equalize _
its productive power, . eo

Rotation of crops is, I believe, practised so far that béjrd usually follows
moth. Perhaps the best-known division of land is into uneven (ti6t) and level
( sot dhartf), the latter being much more valued than the former.

Land being unlimited the holdings are Jarge; butI cannot tell how -
large a holding is equivalent to Rs. 8 a month,
nor have I much to ‘record regarding ‘the land
tennres. But I have heard it remarked that in consequence of land being so
plentiful, and owing to the wretched revenue system worth so little, there is no -
struggle for shares (biswds) as in the adjoining British districts. - Cultivators
sometimes take Jeases from the village proprietors, rent being probably fixed
yearly, To oust a cultivator before the succession of crops is complete, that
is after he has prepared the land for bajra by growing moth upon it, is,.I believe, |
thought unjust. . Loo
The rents charged by the Bhimfa thakurs are realized. from the
cultivators in various ways. - In one village.
where enquiries were made the-“ begars” (or village
servants at the beck of the thaékur) paid a third of their crop; while the Jats
paid from Rs. 10 to Rs. 20 a family, according to the number of a household, -
in addition toa third of the crop. Ten rupees for seventy or eighty bighds is a _
common rent for Banias to charge when they hold villages in farm, while. _
smaller village proprietors often take but one-fourth the crop, or, if they supply
plough cattle, half. oo
The Darbar’s revenue rates, which are really rent rates, in fiscal villages —
are Rs. 10 fora hundred bfghas (about twenty-four acres) from Jats, and a rupee
or two Jess from Réjpits, Bréhmans, and other favored castes. Where --
there is a spring harvest, from one-fourth to one-seventh is taken.’ This © -
of course does not free the land for the autumn crop, whereas in Tibi. land
it occasionally bears twice in the year. In some villages the darbar takes-
one-fourth of the autumn crops from Jats. It must, however, be remembered
that these rates are supplemented by other irregular taxes whenever a good
-year makes it possible for the villages to pay more.

It is a remarkable fact that the domestic animals of Bikanir are
generally either finer or more serviceable than

CarriR. those of any other part of India.

The horses, if not fine, are strong and. wiry ; and I have known a very
ordinary-looking mare carry its rider eighty miles through sand: one days an
fourty the next, and then withouta rest continue moderate daily journeys: \ eile
fed mding camels will do even more than this. They do not, however, t rive

well out of the sandy tract. a - _—

Except by thie officials who plunder the people,

The piece-goods and other local trade is chiefly in the hands of Agegar-
Alas, and there can be no further profitable investments for realized capital.
Formerly, by grants of lands at favorable rates, immunity from eustom dues,
and other encouragements, merchants have been induced by the Raja of the
day to settle in new towns and invest money in building and agriculture;
and there is no doubt that if the merchants could trust: the Darbér, many
would be glad to take grants of waste lands, attract cultivators, and. thus, by
diminishing the vast unutilized tracts, to contribute in the best. possible way
to the prosperity of the state. Oo
At present the wealth brought into the country by the. merchants whose
houses of business are at the great seats of commerce is expended chiefly. in
great entertainments of Bréhmans or of the castes of the owneis, or it Is
hoarded in the shape of jewels and ornaments. The. Darbar would often
gladly borrow a few lakhs, but its eredit is so bad that speculators will now
rarely lend to it. . a a

books which had Jain for generations untouched to be opened, and examination
showed that some had actually turned to dust from lapse of time, though insects
had been kept from them. Many of these books were bought by Réjé Antip
Singh when the Emperor Aurangzeb was destroying every Sanserit work he
could Iay his hands on, At Bfkdnir they were safe from everything but time.
In the Jain monastery, Achérajf Gatchké, when I expressed a hope that the
Srf Puji or Jain priest would not object to his books being rendered available
to the public, his reply was; Examine them by all means; the more you
work a well the purer the water.” At Bhatner in A. D. 1847 General
Cunningham saw number of palm-leaf manuscripts believed to be Jain.
They are not now there. Itis desirable that their fate should be discovered.
The completion of the catalogues above referred to probably depends upon
the sustained interest shown in it.*

During the latter part of the late Mahdrajé Sardar Singh’s life-time the -
' , administration of justice was carried, on in aryery

“Joose and unsatisfactory. manner, ” ief per

_, The system of selling offices, and of allowing officials to absorb wealth by

revenue or judicial administration from being satisfactory. Now and then‘a -
minister, cither from virtue or superstition, swears that place shall bring him
no seeret gain, but the community think a native is quite virtuous enough if
in cases entrusted to him he favors and takes money from one party only, and
that from the party who should justly win and does win. ‘Since the death of

disregarded, suflicient food was supplied. Their place of confinement, called -
 Netdsar,” has been for many generations the state prison. Latterly,

confined insa place called the “ Dharmpura,” but none of these three places
deserve the name of jail: they are simply lock-ups.

Béorfs, Minas, and Thoris are the criminal classes of the state. They are

who supply them with horses and camels and
conceal the plunder, The principal temples, the residences of saints, and the

until the time of Mahfrija
Meer Gaj Singh, who ruled in Bik&nfr from 1746 toe
- 1787. Prior to his time the current coin was the
Farrikh Shahi rupec. It is said that the imperial sanction was given to the
establishment of the mint at Bikanfr, but probably at that period it was not
thought worth asking for. However, the coins were struck in the name of the
Emperor Muhammad Sh&h Alam, and continued to be so until ten years ago,
when for the name of the Dehli Emperor was substituted Queen Victoria—
* Aurdngfrff Hind?’ Silver and copper coin are both struck, but the former
alone can be considered current, as the latter vary in value and the place of
their manufacture is distinct from the mint. Rupees have been struck under
four Mabérijés, and the “ Gaj Sh&hi,” “Surat Sh&hi,” “Ratan Shahi,” and
 Saidér Shahf” are all at present current. Each coin bears the device of the
reigning Mahéraji, as well as of those who preceded him since the establish-
ment of the mint. A full account of the process of coining is on record in the
Sujéngarh Ageney Office.

here are no statistics available to show the precise number of villages in
former times, but Bik&, whose dominions had © .
CO about the same limits as those of the state as it | -
now is, is said in round numbers to have had 3,000 villages under his sway:
the present number is 1814, and there is no doubt that many have been

would greatly increase the population and improve the revenue. .
The prevailing wind in the latitude of the city is from the west, £0.
much so that the inscriptions on old memorial ~
; _. pillars in the villages of the country are, owing
to the action of drifting sand, much more worn when they have a western
aspect than when they face any other quarter. The only year for which there -
is any meteorological record is 1872, during which a rainfall of 10°10 inches
was reported by Captain Burton. .
Guinea-worm is one of the commonest complaints, and in the city of
DIsnases. Bikanfr syphilis is very prevalent. Few, it is said,
. _ whether men or women, have escaped it. Cholera
- occasionally visits the state, and a detachment of the Deoli force at Sujéngarh
suffered severely from ‘ Beri beri or the bad sickness of Ceylon ” in 1868.

« apparently tending to the contrary, Bikénfr cannot but be regarded as, for
“natives at least, 2 moré than ordinarily healthy locality. This has been -
“already generally attributed to free perflation of pure air arid to good water, -
“but something must be credited to the population being generally well off?)
**in consequence of the various profitable employments which exist, and to the
« yeadiness of the poorer classes to fall back onthe famine foods which the un-
« tilled tracts afford. Doubtless to natives of some other parts of India the dry -
“ climate of Bfkdnir, and to the European the frightfal intensity of the. hot- |
‘t weather, would render the climate extremely trring—even natives dying from -
“ sonstroke and fever at this period.* The dispensary returns, both: for the -

other places, and although both intermittent and remittent are common:

PART Ii.

THE DARBAR, ARISTOCRACY, AND OFFICIAL CLASSES.

The Mab&rdjf of Bikfnfr claims to be head of the Réthor clan, and
therefore to take precedence of Jodhpir on the
grounds set, forth at pages 12 and 18 of the Ilistory.
Whether the claim is just or not I will not undertake to say.

Each state has at different times taken possession of the capital of the other,
Jodhpér has most often been in a position to act as aggressor, and, in addition
to the one successful invasion, has several times besieged the fort of Bikanfr
without success. The British Government has allowed to cach state the same
salute, riz., seventeen guns. The part the Bikdnir State played in the time of
Akbar, when in acknowledgment of his services in Gujrat or in other ways the

ror bestowed on Rao R&i Singh extensive territories, and it is said the
title of Réja, together with the wealth that the Dakhan commands brought
into the country, raised the pretensions and expenditure of the darbir to
such an extent that even now the effect is felt, and extravagant habits have
long prevailed which for generations have kept the state in debt. Insignia
of honor, called “ mahi maratib,” were twice conferred on the Bikanir chief
by the Mighals—first, on Antp Singh after a successful campaign in the
Dakhan; and second, on Gaj Singh for services to the empire. The insignia
were not identical, and the precise degree of honor conferred I do not know.
In Akbar's time Ri Singh of Bikénir had a place on the list of man-
eabdars higher than that of any other Hindi, except the ruling family
of Amer.

It may be taken for granted that every Bikénir chief will first aim at
being recognized by the British Government ag
head of the Rithors, and failing that at avoiding
any settlement of the question of precedence as regards himself and the

When intercourse between the British Government and Bikénir was

Besides these three, there were, and are, no thékurs with more than thirty

villages, and but a few who held more than a dozen; but Biddwats, whose

country extends along part of the Marwar and Shekhdwati borders ‘(see “
7 3 . ° aw see “ Su-
sANGATIT ”) , and the Sringot Bikds, near the north-east boundary, were formid-

The Bidawats especially are addicted to plundering.

Mahardajé Strat Singh, great-vrandfather of the present chief, was a prince
of much vigor, and in the early part of his reign he was successful against
his enemies abroad, and reduced his thékurs to subjection at home. Chard
was taken, custom dues were exacted from the Bidawats, many of the refractory
were deprived of their estates, and some were imprisoned and put to death.
But after a time the ousted owners, with the aid of others, recovered their estates, —
plundered the country, and defied the Darbar. At length the assistance of
the British Government was solicited, the treaty of 1818 was concluded, and a’
British force entered Bikaénir. It restored some twelve forts to the Darbar,
extending from near Hissar to Sijangarh, and entirely ‘re-established the
Darbar’s authority. Chini and Baéhddran were both confiscated, but for many: -
years there was no approach to order throughout the state. British troops had
to co-operate with the Darbar against the outlaws, and assistance in various
ways was accorded.

In 1884 Major Foster was established in Jhinjini in command of the
Shekhéw4ti Brigade, of which one troop was composed of Bidéwats, and -
¢owards the expenses of which Bikénir contributed Rs, 22,000 annually.

Major Foster’s efficient force and his vigorous action prevented the
malcontent thékurs of Bikénir from using Shekhdwati any longer as a. refuge
and a lurking place, while his neighborhood vastly increased the control-
ling power of the then chief, who was able to raise the sum first levied

frora the thékurs in lien of service by Mahdréj4 Strat Singh, after his treaty. |.

with the British Government. oe,
When Major Foster’s brigade was removed from Shekhéwati, the- Darbar

had become too strong to need direct assistance from Government’; so that when
in 1856 an attempt was made by the Chiird thékur to- recover his estate by’
force, he was easily driven away and eventually captured. wo
The thékurs of Bikinfr have thus been subjected; the once powerful
nobles of Chiiré and ‘Bahddrd4n now hold but three or. four villages and reside
constantly under the. eye of’ the Darbar. Mahdjan has been deprived of a
third of his estate, taxes were multiplied, and lands transferred at the pleasure. -
=== PDFPAGE 0132 ===
(m3

of the Mahardid, and all this without more than a very few thikurs holding
grants again venturing to resort to the usual expedient of leaving their state
and openly plandering in it from another,

So far as I know, there is no state in Réjpatdnd in which the old fendal
tenure has so nearly passed away, and the power of the chief is so absolute as
in Bikénfr. But there isa limit to endurance, and to avoid the retrogression
which would be so great an evil, the Darlsir certainly onght to make and
keep to settlements* with the thikurs similar to the one mentioned at
page “2, instead of constantly deepening the discontent by unreasonable
additions to their burdens. They furnish no treops to the Darbér, but pay
“rakm” or a sum in commutation of service.

This “rakm,” though spoken of as commutation for service, included also
a. protection tax, called “rakhwali,” levied at a time when thékurs required
support from the inreads of Sikhs and others. ‘ Rekh’’ is the term for com-
mutation of service, and “rakm” is “rekh” plas “rakhwali,”

The thakurs of Bikanfr are divided into—

(1).—Those descended from Bik, consisting of nine important fami-
lies and known generally as Bikas.

(2).—Those from Bids, Bika’s brother, consisting of six important
families known as Brdawats.

{3).—Those from Karmsi, Bikd’s less distinguished brother, one
small family called Karmsiots.

(4).—Those from Kandhal, Bik4’s uncle and chief supporter, consist-
ing of three great familes known as Randhalots, See “Cnvrt
and Bauipris.”

(3).—-Those from Mandhal and RGpji, two other uncles of Biké, less
distinguished than Kdndbal. Their successors are known
respectively as Mandblawats and Rupdwats, neither very
important.

(6)}.—The Bhétis who possessed their estates before Biké’s time, and
submitted to him or his descendants. They consist of Ravlots
which have nine branches, and Pugalia which have four.

(i) -—Misoflaneous Réjpat families of various clans: twenty-three
in all. , .

The above are all “ tézimi,” or entitled to a place in Darbér.
The thakurs of Bikinfr in many cases retain their pristine manlines=: and
vigor, which are probably kept alive Ly ‘border
fights, by the necessity of looking to their own
resources for protection rather than to the authority of the Darbir, xttenusted
as it is by extension over a vast tract, and lastly by whst they mill thet
“game of dacoity and cattle-lifting.” ,
Every one who has the means possesses a small fort, wise
by a rampart of sand supported by “‘phog” twigs, called a

wife of a rich Bania, is then hurried off without‘delay in a direction likely”

home to him, he seldom suffers any penalty beyond having to restore the.
stolén goods, and he enn sometimes venture to refuse to do even that. The

worse than the discomfort of frequent and rapid travelling on the back:
of a camel. She is certain of being treated with the utmost respect consistent

With her security. If, 100, at the crisis of the exciting game the- Rajpat

dacoit comes into ‘collision with a Rajpat lady, that is if she chooses to -
give refuge in her own apartments to. the victims of his violence, he will

usually give way and resign the prize; jin fact, the typical Réjput is

the most gentlemanly brigand under the sun. I do not mean to say

that all thikurs are without seruples as regards plundering: some .no doubt

disapprove of if, and even an average thakur often thinks it improper to

weak neighbors, The thdkur of the desert is of convivial habits, and when

a feast is given the guests usually make a night of it, and can attend to no

business, however, pressing, till noon the next day. Their inferior servants are

nearly always slaves (see “ Orricran cLasses,” “Suaves”).

The menials are hereditary household slaves, called, “chelas.” They are,
SravEs I believe, never sold by Rajptit families of distine-

. tion, though they often form a part of a bride’s
dowry. When not the children of slaves, they have usually been purchased
in times of famine from their starving relations. Their work is light, and.
they are generally well treated and sometimes placed in positions of high -

. trust; but thdkurs, especially the inferior ones, occasionally act with much |
cruelty towards their slaves as well as their other dependents. ‘ Chelas”
who have fled from their masters are to be met with in British territory,
where they often assume the caste of their former owners. The term
“chela” signifies disciple rather than slave, and was applied to household
.servants by the large-minded Akbar as the following extract will show.
Whether in this use of the word the Rajpiits were taught by Akbar or he by
them I cannot at present say:— —
“ His Majesty from religious motives dislikes the name ‘ bandah’ or slave,
“ for he believes that mastership belongs to no one but God. He therefore
“calls this class of men chelahs, which Hindi ‘term’ signifies a ‘faithful
'« disciple.’ Various meanings attach to the term ‘slave.’ First, that which
*neople in general mean bya slave. Some people obtain power over such
“as do not belong to their sect and sell and buy them. The wise look upon
« this as abominable (Blochmann’s translation of din Ahbart, p. 254)".

PART IV.

DISTRICTS, TOWNS AND VILLAGES.

The northern parganas of the Bfkinfr State lie along the bank of the Sotra

. or Hakré, in the dry bed of which wells are
Nontaeey Panaivas, dug and the best water in the region obtained.

The head-quarters of the Parganas Andpgath, Sarddrgarh or Alwdua, Sifrat-
garh and Haniméngarh, or Bhatuer and ‘Tib{, are all on the bank of the
ancient river. ‘The inhabitants of the region are Bhit{s Pirzdd&s (see “ Pastonat
prorL.e”), Sodas Réhts, Johiyas, and Bhattis (sce below under “ Buatven ”).
The country is bare and desolate toa degree: towards the east sardly and undu-
lating, elsewhere often firm and level. In spite of the difficulty about water,
the grazing is excellent and the number of cattle possessed by the people

The Sotra occasionally flows. General Cunningham speaks of the water some-
times coming roaring down, but its waters are soon absorbed in the desert.
The Naiwél is another old river-bed lying nearly parallel to, and a few
miles north of, the Sotra, but water never I believe flowa in it. Other old
channels there are, and so great was the united body of water which must at
some period have flowed down them that,they are conjectured to have borne

the ancient Satlhj.

The grazing in this pargana is in parts very good; but water, which is

are scarcely any masonry buildings in the pargana, and no villages with a
population of five hundred persons. Réjpdts and Musalmdns (the latter are
called Sindh) are the most numerous, .

The old bed of the Kagar, which crosses the district and on which the
fort and bead-quarters station is situated, sometimes contains water in the |
rains, after which some wheat and barley is produced. vo
Labdnés, J&ts, Réths and Johyas are the principal inhabitants.
_ Sajj4 is an important article of manufacture in about twenty. villages to.
the north of the district. os
The Bhatner fort has attained great historical celebrity from its position,
being in the route of invasion from Central Asia to India. ©
I am informed by Mr. Oliver, Jate Deputy Commissioner of Sirsa, that
Bhatner, Abor, Bhatindé, and Sirsa, situated at the angles of a nearly square
figure with a side about fifty miles long; each had a fort on the same plan and
of the same dimensions, and thus was formed a “quadrilateral” in the path
‘of any invader from the north-west. , ot
The “ Lértkh Hind” speaks of Bhatner having been taken by Mahmitid of
Ghazni in 1001; and Tod speaks of its having been attacked by Timtr. It
seems probable that he left a Tartar Chagitai noble in charge ‘of it, who was
expelled by Bhattis from Mérot and Phulra under one Bers! ; but whether the
place took its name originally from them or from one Raja Bharat is 2 moot
‘point (Hissar Gazetteer). General Cunningham, who, with the late Colonel
Jackson, was employed as Commissioner in A. D. 1847 to determine the north-
east boundary of Bhdwalpur and, Bikénir, states that Bhatner, was taken by
Khets{ Kondhalét in Sambat- 1854 (A. D. 1527) from Sada Chéyal Rajput,
of which clan no mention is made by-Tod. In Sambat 1606 (A. D. 1549) —
these dates do not exactly tally with those of the Kiyént—Mirza Kamréhy
‘brother of Hiimaytin, assaulted and took the fort, on which occasion Khets!

with five thousand Réjputs was slain, though Kémrén was -subsequentls
=== PDFPAGE 0146 ===
a (193)

defeated by Réo Jeted of Blkéntr, Firoz Chéyat had meanwhile recovered
the fort, and the Réo therefore sent his son, Thakur Sf, to retake it. The rest
of the Commissioner’s abstract of the history of Bhatner, which was derived
from two different Persian manuscripte, one of which was obtained from a
Saiyad, the descendant of the ancient K4zés of Bhatner, tallies generally with
the “Kiydnt,” with which the above too does not materially differ. The Com-
missioners examined the earliest decd produced referring to Bhatucr, namely, the
imperial firmén under which Réi Singh of Bikinir held the pargana from

When Réjé Antp Singh visited the parganain Sambat 1742, a bard,
one Karam Sfndu, recited a poem before him, which specified the extent of the
pargana and gave the names of the villages inhabited at that time as 211 in
number. The bard received in rent-free grant the village of Ludfna on the
old bed of the Sotra, and his descendant produced the poem before the Com-
missioners. General Cunningham has allowed me to examine the poem, which
describes the pargana as reaching six “jojans” (42 miles) north of Bhatocr fort
twelve “jojans”” (84 miles) west, 22 kos (44 miles) cast, and 12 kos (24 miles)
south, ‘The sites of a very large number of the villages are still known.

The Johiyas are Réjpits (and not Jats as supposed by Tod) of a very
ancient and powerful stock indeed, once known as Yaudheyas, who probably
contended with Alexander, before whose time they were, it is believed, estab-
lished on the banks of the Satlaj.. Yaudheya coins as old as the first century
of the Christian era have been found near that river (Cunningham’s dncient
Geography, Vol. I, p. 245). The Johiyas are by no means now extinct, as
Tod imagined; many of them are Musalmfns, but some in the Sirs4
district, Mr. Oliver tells me, aro still Hinds. According to General Cun-
ingham* they formerly held much of the country west and south-west of
Bhatner, and their chief places were Khdrb4ra, Sab&nkot, and Badopul, which
the R&thors took from them. There are three sections of the tribe—the
Admera and Lakvira, who are settled on the banks of the Satlaj, and the
Madhera, who, together with the representative of the House of Madhu, the
founder of the clan, are for the most part subjects of the Bikénfretate. The
boundary of these two divisions is still the bank of the old Satlaj. .

The Bhattis cf Bhatner are believed to be Bhattfs converted to Islamism,
and to be descended from Jnisal, who founded Jaisalmfr and who is said ta
have been the ancestor, by a Jeft-handed marriage, of the Sikhs of Patfilé,
Jind, and Nébhé.

The Réhts also are said to be converted Réjptits; they are of four clans,
two of which claim to be Chohéns, one Tuar, and one Saros. In His-de
they are known as Pachidas, and their name“ Raith” signifies eruel, Ther
were great maranders and are now pastoral (/issfe Gazetteer),

The principal villages of the pargana are—

(1) Ratangarh, (2) Rabia, (3) Bitia,
(4) Rénésar, (6) Ghasoli.

The headquarters of the district of the same name is said to have been
Onaed founded by Chohar Jat two hundred and fifty years ago.:

, Chohar possessed the other twelve villages of the district, |
as well, and paid revenue to the Imperial Subadér of Hiss4r. ..He is said to
have been killed at village Sdniim in Reni, and to have been succeeded by his
brother, Dildwar, who fell fighting with Kandhal’s descendant, Mélde. Malde’s
suecessor, Khushfél Singh, is said to have built the fort in Sambat 1796
(A. D. 1739), and to have added to the prosperity of the town by giving
refuge to the Banyias of Fatahpir when they fled from the extortions of *

Nahar Singh Shekhéwat, the conqueror of the K4im Khénfs rulers of Fatahpur

in the present Sikar territory.

It is remarkable as containing a strip of country ten koa long by four

water is near, often very near, the surface; and nowhere in the pargana is it
necessary in search of water to delve down to the depths at which it is found
in other parts of the state, for it is always to be obtained within a hundred
and fifty feet from the level of the ground. Another noticeable feature is the
eight or nine rocky hills scattered over the district, which relieve the cye
wearied with the expanse of sand or sand hillocks. In one of these hills, near
the village of Dariba, copper ore is found, but the mine is not rich enough to
repay working it, Two hills of the group near Gopslpdra have shrines upon
them : they are of granite, and attain a height of seven or eight hundred fect
above the plain. Under one of them once nestled the very ancient town of
Dronpiir, celebrated in the early history of the district and said to have been
founded by Drona, the tutor of the Pandds.

Besides the towns and forts about to be mentioned, it is only necessary to
speak of the lake of Chdpar. Like all “ sars” or lakes in the sandy tracts, it
is formed by a depression which receives the drainage of the surrounding
country, and is about three kos long by a kos broad. In its neighborhood salt
deposits are found in a smaller hollow, and about twenty-five tons of salt is

Lime-stone is plentiful in many localities, but it is often soft and does

“ The city is situated in a singularly desolate and slightly clevated spot,
“the soil being hard, stony, and totally unfit for cultivation. Viewed from

‘a fine wall surmounted by many round towers and crowned with the usual
“ Indian battlements, So imposing is its appearance that, when approached
“in A. D, 1808 by Elphinstone’s mission, there were disputes among his
“ followers whether it or Debli was the more extensive. Some Ingh houses
and temples rising above the ramparts and the striking outline of the lofty
part add to the impressive spperrance of the place.

The wall is three and a half miles in circuit, built wholly of stone with
“fine gates and three cally ports, It is six feet, thick and from fifteen to
“ thirty feet high, including a parapet six feet high and two feet thick, the
* breadth of the terreplein varying from two to four fect. The names of the
“ gates aro—

* the city being intersected by deep ravines, which have broken up the whole
plain in that quarter, As the soilis ‘kankar’? or calcarcous conglomerate
“ intermingled with siliccous pebbles, the sides of the ditch, though not lined
* with masonry, are nearly perpendicular; the depth is about fiftcen fect, the
* breadth twenty feet, the interval between the wall and the ditch from twenty
to thirty yards; but ia some places the excavation has been quite filled up
“and obliterated.” .

Dr. Moore, already quoted, remarks as follows regarding the interior of

“To the north and north-east within the walls there are large open -
“‘ spaces, not yet builé over, or with only small houses here and there.
“ Penetrating further, the more densely-populated portion of the town is
“reached, and this, from the extreme irregularity of the streets, lanes, and
“ spaces defies any clear description. Acquainted as I am with all the
“ capitals of Réjptitiné and with most of the large provincial cities, I can
* safely state there is not one vieing with Bik4nfr as regards the grotesque
“irregulavity of its thoroughfares. After perambulating the city by both
“ highways and bye-ways, the conclusion was formed that the better way of -
“attempting any description is commencing from the centre, or at least -
“ from a Jain temple near enough the middle to deserve the title of central. -
‘From this temple, or from very near the spot, five larger streets branch —
off, which, following a tortuous course and often losing themselves in other _
“ streets or open spaces, ultimately convey the bold experimentalist towards one’
“or other of the large gateways. The crooked alleys, open spaces, and
“ narrow lanes between these streets can only be described as a confused mass,
presenting the irregularity which one might imagine they would assume had
“some Cyclops east them from the skies, and then rolled them about at .

¢ random with the wand of disorder.”

“from the size of a pea to that of an egg, composed of quartz. and:
“ not round,’ yet with surfaces and angles <0 smooth. as . to et alo a
“ the idea that they must at some time have’ been exposed to the action of
“ Tunning water. Carter, the geologist, has, I believe, expressed the opinion,
“that the whole of this semi-desert portion of . Western ‘India did at.
‘ some time form the bed. of an ocean, extending from the present shores ‘of
“ the sea to the line of the Arivalli Range, and the geological. character.
“ istics shown to exist by the deep: wells’ of Bikénir would seem to ‘support: -
“ this opinion. On this point I may also observe that I found an unmistak-
“able fossil-shell mark on a stone of the wall of the old fort. built by Bikéji,
‘ and before alluded to. Although no companion marks were seen in other .
‘stones examined, yet a more minute and scientific search than I could afford
“would probably prove successful. Se
“As would naturally be supposed after a knowledge of its source, the.
“water of Bikénir, if not plentiful, is most excellent in ‘quality. ‘It is cer- -
“tainly somewhat hard from excess of lime derived from the stratum of
“¢Kankar,’ but it is otherwise pure and good, and when drawn from the wells -
“often presents a temperature of 85° Fahrenheit. Neither does it contain
‘organic impurities, or at least only to a small extent, for, contrary to what
“occurs in almost every other part of India with which I am acquainted, the
“wells are carefully covered and’ guarded, perhaps not altogether with. the |
“desire of protecting the wells from the entrance of impurities, but’for the’ -
“existing necessity of preventing accidents to those drawing water, for
“occasionally on the rope (raw hide—here used for strength) breaking, the —
“leathern bucket has rapidly descended, taking with it (owing to somé acci- |
“dental entariglement) the person tending the rope at the mouth. But the.
“ stone coverings placed over all the wells effectually protects them from those
“impurities so often tainting well water, and thus serves two useful purposes,
“ Another cause tending to the purity of the water is the absence of the —
*phishti’s ‘massak’ at Bikdnir. The water is drawn-by bullocks, and it is)”
“then poured into large ‘gharas’ or jars, and-taken away.on camels, or |
“ponies or bullocks, for use. In the cold season it is sold in the city at one
“ pakica pice per ° ghardé,’ or fifty-six ‘gharaés’ per rupee :.in the hot weather if -
‘is much more costly. But it escapes the contamination of the never-opened;
‘and therefere never thoroughly cleansed, bhishtis’ ‘massaks.’ The adoption -
“of this Bikénfr custom elsewhere in barracks or in private houses might ~
“perhaps be conducive to a better water-supply,'and to a diminution of those -
“maladies supposed to be at least sometimes disseminated by the use of.
“impure water. ; oo Z oe
“The Bikdnir water is also further maintained pure by the absence of -
“the custom so prevalent in all other places, of washing on the brink, or even ,
“in the wells from which drinking water is procured. In Bikénir if’a person. _
“3s told he'is dirty, he immediately replies that water is scarce; and it may ©
‘be doubted if the great majority of the inhabitants of Bikdnir ever wash |
“at all, although some, from religious motives, may perform the motions of '
“washing with sand. But this absence of washing, as compared with doing
“so in the wells, is undoubtedly the least of two-evils;‘so the scarcity of -
“water at Bikdnir leads, paradoxical as it may appear, to a better supply, for”
“no such fouling of the wells as hourly witnessed at other places would there .

“be tolerated.” | . yo

The peculiar importance of tanks and wells to Bikanir must-he my ex-
cuse for entering into. such minute details, which, I. believe, will at a future,
period be useful for purposes of comparison.

The sanitary condition of the city is very bad. A native report says:—
“People use earthen vessels as night-stools, and
“empty them into the streets from the house-tops
“ia the morning regardless of the passers-by.” The condition of the streets
after a shower of rain may be conceived, and a certain desire on the part of
the travelled Baniyas for some improvement of this state of things is one
of the few apparent, effects of their knowledge of well-ordered cities, and
since Captain Burton, Political Superintendent, was at Bik&nir, somethine has
been done towards keeping the streets clean .

The fort of Bikinir, which contains the Mahdrajé’s palace, is situated
about three hundred yards from the Kot gate of the
city. ‘The palace buildings towering above the

about forty feet high, and a moat running all round in a direction parallel to
the curtains without following the curve of the bastions. The ditch is thirty
feet. wide at the top but narrow at the bottom, and twenty or twenty-five feet

se,
cd

deep.

The fort was builé in Sambat 1645 by R4jé R4f Singh (vide p. 29). It
has been ineffectually besieged several times but has never been taken, I
believe, though the old fort once was. The palace buildings have been :
raised bit by bit by successive rajds, nearly every one of whom has contributed
something. ——

The elephants and horses are, as is usual in a Rajptit fort, just under the
palace winduws, and one passes partly through the stables in going to visit the
Maharaja.

The old fort, built by Biké, is picturesquely situated on some high rocky

Ola fort ground, surrounded by ravines, outside the southern .
" wall of the city. It is small,-and is now more
a shrine than a fort: within it are the cenotaphs of Biké and his successors,
Liinkaranjf and Jet Singh, together with some of less note. , The place
is called “ Bikaji-ki-Tekri.” Inscriptions record the dates of the funerals
and the number of “satis” at each. The original stones were for the
most part renewed by Mahéréj4 Ratdn Singh, but two of the old ones are
remaining. ' oo, —
The cremation tank of Bikdnfr since the time of Jet Singh; the grandson _
Devi Ku ae of Biké, is situated three miles east of the city. On
evi Kuind or Devi Sagar. each side of the tank are ranged the cenotaphs’ of
twelve chiefs from Kalién Singh to Ratén Singh. Several of them.are fine
buildings, and all are graceful pillared domes: the material isthe red sandstone
of Khari and Makréné marble for the commemorative stones, on which are
sculptured in bass-relief the mounted figure of the chief, and on foot, standing .
in order of precedence before him, the wives, and behind and below him ‘the .
concubines who mounted his funeral pile. The date, names of the dead, and in’

Fort and palnce.

Bikinir. They preserve as an heir-loom their ancestors yard measure. ; _

some cases a line of Sanskrit besides is inscribed. The  sat{s” upto the time
of Gaj Singh average over twelve to each chief, and on Zordwar Singh’s pile

Ut is remarkable that his immediate successor, Gaj Singh, who died in
A.D. 1788, had no * sat,” snd only two persons were subsequently burned
alive. Both of these cremations are remarkable, The one as being the last
distinguished © sat’? in Bikanfr, and consequently the one whose shrine is the
most honored. She was sn Udepdr princess, named Dip Kanwar, the wife of
Maharaji Siirat Singh’s second son Moti Singh, who died in Sambat 1882
{A. D. 1825) People still speak of the courage and devotion her bearing
showed as she wended her way with ancovered face to the Lurning place; and
a numerously attended {air in her honor snnually takes piace in the month of
Bhédon (August) at Dev{ Kénd. The other was that of a man, named
Sangrim Singh, who became “sato”’ with the corpse of R&i Singh in A. D.
1789. When surprise at the fact of a man’s taking the place of « wife is
expressed, one is informed that sex bas nothing to do with the matter—that
itis a question of pare love; and an ancedote is told of a camel following
its dead master to the burning place, Jying down on the pile beside him,
and becoming “sati’? The female“ satis” are far from all being the wives of
those with whom they were barnt. Besides Réals, there are mistresses of the
first grade (khavas), those of on inferior one (pdtrs), attendants of Rénts
{Sahelfs), and bandmaids (kanfzaks} of mistresses.

Not far from the tank there is a palace for the convenience of the chief
and his ladies when they have occasion to attend ceremonies at Devi Kifnd.
The whole zanénf sometimes goca in procession to worship at Devi Kind,
where, too, the tonsure of the chief's sons takes place.

zamin” or tax on the sale of lan towns, (4) “ gnimal” or proceeds of unclaimed property.

“In the forty-fifth year Rai Singh was ordered’to accompan: eile. but ag

“ At the death of the Emperor, Rai Singh was a commander of four-thousand. Jahangtr,
“on his accession, made him » commander of five-thousand. When the Emperor setrout
" for the Panjéb to pursue Khasran, R4i Singh was put in charge of the travelling harem
but on the road he left without order and went to Bikanir. In the second year, when
“Jahangir returned from Kabul, Rai Singh, at the advice of Sharif Khan, presented him-
* self before the Emperor with a fautah round his neck to’ show his Willingness to suffer
“ punishment for his crimes, and was again pardoned. He died in 1021. ‘ .

6 is sons.—(1) Dalpat.—He was a commander of five-thousand. In the thirty-sixth
“year he served in the Sindh war, but was looked upon asa coward. In the forty-fifth
“year, when Akbar was in the Dakhan, Muzaffar Husain: Mirza, in consequence of his
“ differences with Khwajagi Fathullah, had fled ; and Dalpat, under the pretext of following
“him up, had gone to Bikanir and created disturbances. In the forty-sixth year his father ,
“ brought him to his senses. Dalpat asked to be pardoned, and was ordered again to come
to court. ; .

“In the third year of Jahangir’s reign (1017), he appears to have offended the Emperor,
“but at the request of Khén Jahin Lodf was pardoned. After the ‘death of his ‘father
“Dalpat came from the Dakhan to court, was appointed successor, and got the title of
“Rai, although his younger brother (by another mother), Stir Singh, claimed the right
“of succession, which Rai Singh had promised him from affection to his mother. Str
“Singh, however, disgusted Jahangir by the bold way in which he preferred his claim.

“ Dalpat was then ordered to join M. Rustam-i-Safawi, the Governor of Sindh. In the.
“eighth year it was reported to Jahangir that Stir Singh had attacked and defeated his
‘‘ brother, who in consequence had created disturbances in Hisar. HA4shim, the Foujdar
“ of that Sirkdr, caught him and sent him fettered to court, where he was executed as a warn: -
“ing to others. .

“For Dalpat’s son, Mahes Dis, and grandson, Ratan (vide PApisHAnNAMaR, pp. 635,
* 793, 684, 729).

“ After the death of his brother, Stir Singh, he rose to favor. . In histories he is gene-
“rally called Rio Str Singh, a title which he received from Shdbjahin. He died in 1040,
“ He had two sons, Karan and Sater Sl, the former of whom inherited the title of Rao
“ (vide PAdish4hnamah II, page 727).” ‘

The Akbarnimah mentions that the Emperor Humaytin, when contending with Sher
Shab, passed through Bikinfr in 949 H., A. D. 1542 (p. 211, Note, Vol. V, Mus. His.).
When Akbar’s famous minister, Bairam Khan, was after his fall prevented by Maldeo of
Jodhptr from marching on Gujarat, he proceeded from Nagor to Bikanir, where the
zamindaér of that country, R4i Kalian Mal, and his son, Rai Singh, received him with great
kindness, and kept him some days at Bikdnfr, treating him most hospitably (Fabakat,
p. 265, Vol. V, Mus. His.).

The Tabak4t mentions that in 978 H. (A. D. 1570) Akbar went to Négor, where he
received both Chandr Sen of Mérwér and Kalian Mal of Bikdnir. The latter, who vat
accompanied by his son Rai Singh, came to present his tribute. As Kalian Mal was too ja
to sit on horseback, he was allowed to go home, but Rai Singh was kept in attendance on |
His Majesty (p. 335, Vol. V, Mus. Hrs.). Loe

In the second Gujarit war R4i Singh and Bhagwan DAs of Amer were sent with the
advance detachment (p. 361, Vol. V, Mus. His.). . -

his people put to death Muhammad Husain Mirza (mentioned above) who was
(p. 368, Vol. V, Mus. H1s.).

eighty kos in two days (p. 463, Vol. V, Mus, His.). Jd name
